Before an action that may change pages (opening a same-site URL, tapping, going back, key press, scrolling down, swiping, `web_interact`'s key press) it fills in the part of this page's dwell not yet reached, counting the time the model spends thinking and reading the page; typing, clearing, scrolling up and reading do not wait, and going to another website does not wait. How long it dwelled is recorded in this call (`ToolExecution.waitMs` → run record `tool_result.waitMs`, counted as waiting in the summary, and `do_steps` sub-steps also count toward that batch) and is not told to the model separately; it is installed only by the App's tasks (`toolDispatcher(webPacing = …)`), and core tests do not wait by default.
  - Site knowledge's `pace: careful` (this website limits how fast one can browse, see `docs/dev/knowledge-format.md`) makes this website's pages dwell longer: from the second page on 10–16 seconds, and after that each additional page raises the lower and upper bounds by 0.6 / 1 second each, up to 30 seconds. It looks up site knowledge by the current host name (`GuideCatalog.sitePace`, with the same `SiteMatch` site-matching rule as injecting site knowledge) and hands it to `PageDwell` through `toolDispatcher(webSitePace = …)`.
  - The page cap is not set by default (`PagePacing.Limits.pageCap` is null, for the same reason as the App's screen cap). When `pageCap` is given, a task can get at most this many pages from the same website (pages outside the 10-minute window still count), and after reaching it, page-changing actions (opening a same-site URL, tapping, going back, key presses) are not done and report `page_limit`, with an explanation telling the model to answer with what it has already read and, if the user wants to keep looking, to ask via `ask_user`; scrolling down and reading pages work as usual. After the user has answered (answered a follow-up question, returned a takeover, via `Capability.onUserAnswered`) each website counts afresh—no extra parameter is given to the model to raise the cap, and seeing more requires the user's nod. A rejected call is recorded as usual as a `tool_result` in the run record (`error: page_limit`). The cap only governs browsing and does not interrupt batched contacting or applications: outward actions recognized by the irreversibility decision (sending, submitting) are themselves not blocked by the cap (`ActionExecutor` tells the web capability via `Capability.onOutwardStart` before doing it and via `onOutwardEnd` after), and after one has succeeded this website (by registrable domain) has no cap for the rest of this task, not restored even when the user answers, until the batch reaches the website's own limit (`limitReached`), a risk control or the user stops it; dwell and outward-action intervals are as usual. When pacing is off (`PagePacing.Off`) the cap is not set either.
  - **Small movements during the dwell** (`core/screen/uistate/IdleScroll.kt` pure logic, also used by the App's dwell): when the dwell is at least 3 seconds and the action is not done by screen coordinate (tap by number, back, key press, scroll down, open a URL), during the dwell it lightly scrolls the whole page 1–3 times, each by 0.06–0.16 viewports, mostly downward and occasionally a little back (not exceeding the previous downward scroll), with the few scrolls totaling no more than 0.45 viewports and moving no more than 0.3 viewports net; the total dwell time is unchanged. It does not scroll during a dwell before a tap by coordinate or a swipe (the coordinates would not line up). Through the port's `WebPort.idleScroll`: desktop mode uses the wheel with the pointer not moving, otherwise one slow native swipe; it does not move the mouse, hover or tap; it does not scroll when a page dialog is open, the IME is showing, the input field has focus, or the tab is not on the display. How many times it scrolled is only recorded in the run record (`idleScrolls` in the `tool_result` result) and not told to the model; after scrolling, it looks once more before the action as \"before the action\" (`ScreenSession.reprobe`), this small scroll does not count as the action's effect, the screen the model holds is marked stale (`interfaceStale`); the next screen read sees the scrolled position truthfully. It is installed only by the App's tasks (`toolDispatcher(webIdleScroll = …)`), and core defaults to no scroll.
- Risk control: the web barrier judgement (`WebBarrier`), besides rate-limit status codes and human-verification frameworks, also recognizes risk-control block pages with little text (abnormal access, abnormal environment, too many operations, unusual traffic, or titles like "Security notice" "Security check" plus words like abnormal or verification), judges them as needing user handling (access_blocked), and `web_diagnose` hints to hand it to the user and not retry immediately; when the top-level message field (message, msg, error…) of a response fetched by `web_capture` says it is blocked by risk control, the result carries `riskControl` and the same hint.
- Tools: `web_navigate`, `web_observe`, `web_read_page`, `web_list_elements`, `web_tap`, `web_type_text`, `web_clear_text`, `web_scroll`, `web_swipe`, `web_press_key`, `web_back`, `web_tabs`, `web_wait_for`, `web_interact`, `web_downloads`, `web_upload`, `web_capture`, `web_screenshot`, `web_diagnose`, `web_evaluate_js`, `web_script`, `web_site_mode`, `web_keep_open`, plus the vision tools.

### sandbox: Linux sandbox

- A runtime environment based on PRoot and Alpine 3.22, bundled with the APK, unpacked offline in the background when the App starts (`app/cap/sandbox/RuntimePreparer.kt`, `RootfsInstaller.kt`); it re-unpacks automatically after Claire upgrades. The sandbox programs are arm64-only, other devices show "This device is not supported (arm64 required)" and the model is told not to use this group of tools. For the source and build see `tools/sandbox/README.md`.
- One workspace per conversation, `/workspace` in the sandbox, kept across messages; files attached in the chat are placed in `/workspace/inbox/`; `share_file` saves a file to the phone's "Downloads/Claire" and shows it in the conversation as a file card; when the card is tapped, web pages, Markdown, PDF, images and text-like files open in the App's viewer page (`FileViewerActivity`, see §15), and the rest are handed to an App in the system that can open them.
- Online by default; only after turning off "Allow scripts to access the network" in Settings → Advanced → Files and compute environment does it go offline; when the device cannot isolate the network, it can only run with "Allow scripts to access the network" on (`AndroidSandbox`, the hint in `AdvancedSection`). Each task has a cumulative runtime budget, and the same command failing repeatedly is stopped (`core/cap/sandbox/SandboxGuard.kt`).
- **Network proxy**: it uses Settings › Advanced and other settings' "Network proxy" (see §12 \"Network proxy\"); this page does not show it again. It is given only to commands allowed to access the network (`AndroidSandbox` passes empty when offline), and put into `http_proxy`/`https_proxy`/`all_proxy` (one each in upper and lower case) and `no_proxy` (`LocalHosts.NO_PROXY`: this machine, LAN subnets and suffixes go direct) through `ProcRunner.env`; `https://` proxies are only usable by commands here.
- **Space used**: a group further down the same page (`SandboxUsageReader`, `app/cap/sandbox/SandboxUsage.kt`): how much the total, the runtime environment, skills and conversation workspaces each take, then lists workspaces by conversation (by conversation title, with deleted conversations written as "Deleted conversation", largest first). Sizes are counted by ordinary files in the directory tree, without following links (`AndroidSandbox.treeBytes`). Tapping a row first confirms and then clears that workspace, and the last row clears all; like deleting a conversation it deletes by handle through the file gate, without following links, holding the tree lock's exclusive permit (`SandboxDirs.exclusiveWrite`, used for deleting and replacing a whole directory tree), mutually exclusive with commands, import and reset, and only after getting the permit does it check whether the conversation is busy (when clearing all it checks again before deleting each one); while a large directory is being deleted all conversations' sandbox commands wait; a conversation with a task running or queued states "Cannot clear right now" and is skipped when clearing all. The runtime environment and skills come with the App and are not cleared here (to prepare from scratch use "Reset runtime environment"). The phone storage is not mounted in the container.
- Tools: `run_command`, `run_bundled_script` (runs scripts bundled with knowledge and skills, see `docs/dev/skill-scripts.md`), `read_file`, `write_file`, `search_files`, `share_file`.
- **User terminal**: the user can open a terminal of their own from the conversation's long-press menu or the workspace row in Settings, and run commands in the same conversation workspace. It goes through `SandboxPort.openShell` / `AndroidSandbox.openShell`: the launcher gives `/bin/sh` a pseudo-terminal with `--pty`, with no time limit, taking `SandboxTree.lease` (reclaimed when resetting or clearing the workspace, stopping the whole process tree); it does not connect the phone bridge, so the `mika` command is unavailable in the terminal. Sessions are managed per conversation by `app/terminal/TerminalHub.kt` (at most two globally), and `TerminalSession` keeps the latest 1 MiB of output for a reattached UI; the UI is a WebView + xterm.js (MIT, resources in `app/src/main/assets/terminal/`) in `app/ui/terminal/TerminalWebView.kt`: output is written into xterm in 64 KiB base64 chunks, and input goes back to `TerminalSession` through `Bridge`; xterm's `onResize` calls `SandboxShell.resize`, letting mikarun change the pty with `TIOCSWINSZ` through the `--pty-size` file (the kernel sends SIGWINCH), so rotating the screen or the IME popping up can re-lay it out. OSC 52/8 are swallowed, and page navigation is always blocked. The terminal does not go through `ActionExecutor`, does not write run records, the evidence ledger or the conversation, and does not provide any tools to the model; the network state is frozen when the session starts, with the same settings as `run_command`.
- Commands in the sandbox can call the current task's phone tools with `mika phone <operation> '<json args>'` (`app/cap/sandbox/SandboxBridge.kt`, `BridgeProtocol.kt`): each command run gets a randomly named local socket and a one-time token, and which are open is declared by the tools themselves (`ToolInfo.sandboxCallable`: phone capability tools are open by default, vision tools are not; new phone tools need no list change), and `<operation>` is the tool name without `phone_` (`type`, `clear`, `key`, `launch`, `dump` are shorthand for `phone_type_text`, `phone_clear_text`, `phone_press_key`, `phone_launch_app`, `phone_list_elements`). Each operation still goes back to the single entry point through `SubActions` and is written to the run record, with the same timeouts and limits as calling directly (`phone_wait` / `phone_event` at most 30 seconds, `phone_system_command` 10 seconds). There are also `mika share` and `mika progress`.

### Vision

With a model that can see images (§12), it provides `phone_describe`/`web_describe` (describe the interface) and `phone_find_visually`/`web_find_visually` (locate by looking at the image, `core/screen/vision/Locator.kt`: first find exactly in the control tree, and only when not found let the model frame it, then check the name; a model whose locating accuracy has not been verified needs supporting evidence, but when this screen's tree is unusable or is missing a part and what is sought is not in the tree, there is nothing to compare against, so the frame is handed over anyway, marked `unverified`, and after tapping the effect speaks). Describing the interface reads the whole screen and writes a large paragraph once, several times slower than locating: the prompt only lets it be used when you cannot tell what page it is, and the description requirement (`roles/describe.md`) also asks only for a brief four paragraphs—at most 15 lines of interactive elements, a few characters each, no text that is not a control, only the first few items of a long list, and at most two lines of hints. The screenshot tool is provided only when the main model can see images. Graphic captchas are not solved by itself; they are handed to the user.

### Video

Video capability (`core/cap/video/`, App side `app/media/AndroidVideoPort.kt`) is installed only in tasks started on the phone: `video_info` reads duration, size and whether there is an audio track; `video_frames` samples 1–12 frames evenly (JPEG with the long side at 1280), handed to the main model as images when it can see images, and only metadata when it cannot. Sampled frames are only a sample, and the prompt requires it not to say "I've finished watching" or "I heard it". Decoding is done directly on the handle already verified by the workspace gate (`app/media/VideoProbe.kt` wraps `GatedFile` as a `MediaDataSource`), without copying to the cache first and without reading the whole thing into memory. `video_save` saves into "Movies/Claire" (`VideoExporter`, MediaStore, with a confirmation card first by default). `phone_share_video` goes the same way as sharing an image: `ShareFileExporter` copies while reading and writing into "Downloads/Claire/share", then uses `ACTION_SEND` to open the other App's share page, without tapping send for the user. A chat card's cover likewise takes one frame on the handle; when tapped to play, a copy is made through the gate into `cache/videoplay/` (only the latest one is kept, cleared at start), handed read-only to the system player through a FileProvider.

`video_generate` (offered only when the user has selected a provider and model under "Models › Video models" and turned it on; at most 1 per task by default) submits an asynchronous task, polls, and writes the result while downloading into `cache/video-gen/` and then into `/workspace/out/video/`; the generated video is saved into the conversation with this turn and drawn as a card. Progress (submitted, queued, generating) is shown on the task card in the user's language. When the first-frame image is too large it is first scaled to a JPEG with the long side at 2048 before sending.

Video generation is a capability declared by providers, and not every one has it. In `core/model/VideoBackend.kt`, one `VideoBackend` declares one protocol: `serves(baseUrl)` recognizes which provider addresses it accepts (only the addresses written in the official documentation; relay and custom addresses are not accepted), `capabilities(model)` gives what each model can do (duration range, aspect ratio, resolution, whether a first frame can be supplied, whether the ratio follows the image when a first frame is supplied, whether sound can be requested; a model it cannot recognize gets the part all models support), `create(VideoEndpoint)` builds a `VideoModel` adapter; `VideoBackends.all` is the registry, currently only Volcano Ark Seedance (declared in `ArkSeedance.kt`, the adapter in `ArkSeedanceModel.kt`, and this vendor's details are only in these two files). On the App side, `VideoProviders` in `settings/VideoSelection.kt` filters providers by the registry and checks the stored choice: the settings page lists only providers that can generate video, and the duration, aspect ratio and resolution options come from the capabilities of the model filled in; the stored choice (`VideoChoice`: provider, model, protocol ID) becomes `Stale` when the provider was deleted or the address was changed so that it no longer speaks that protocol, and is not cleared (it still works if the address is changed back), the settings page explains the reason, `ProviderStore.videoModel` does not build a model, the task then does not have this tool, and the request is not sent to an address that does not speak this protocol. Adding another provider only needs writing an adapter and a declaration and registering them into `VideoBackends.all`; the tools, `AndroidVideoGenerationPort` and `TaskRun` need no change (the provider must first pass the "only mainstream, trusted" review).

Billing safety: a submit request is not retried automatically (OkHttp's connection-failure retry is turned off), and if the connection drops after the request is sent it reports `VideoModelError.Unconfirmed` (it does not know whether it was created); polling has an overall time limit (30 minutes by default), occasional network / 5xx / rate-limit errors are tolerated a few times, and a single hiccup does not abandon a task already being billed; when submitting it carries the shortest expiry the provider allows (Ark's `execution_expires_after` is 1 hour), so a task abandoned locally does not run much later. After submission, when it stops waiting due to cancellation, a local timeout or an error, it makes a best effort to send one cancellation (Ark DELETE: only queued ones can be cancelled and are no longer billed; one that is generating cannot be cancelled and will run to completion and be billed). The per-task allowance is refunded only when it is certain no money was spent (the provider has not yet accepted the task, or clearly reports the task failed); timeouts, network loss, download failure and unconfirmed submission after acceptance are not refunded, and the prompt also requires the model not to retry automatically. The result is downloaded using the temporary link the provider gives, without the provider's Key and request headers; on cancellation the connection is dropped immediately, and both the cache file and the half-written workspace file are deleted.

### Subtasks

`spawn_agent` (`core/general/SpawnAgent.kt`, `core/run/ChildRuns.kt`) dispatches a child task to look into a bounded question: web and sandbox only, read-only, with nobody present, 15 steps by default (the model can pass `maxSteps`, 1–40), cannot ask follow-up questions, cannot send outward, cannot write memory, cannot create schedules, cannot dispatch further child tasks, and has no knowledge and skill lookup tools. The result has a length cap (4,000 characters by default, the model can pass `maxResultChars`, 500–8,000; out-of-range values are pulled back into range, and non-integers are rejected as a parameter error; when truncated the result writes `truncated` and the original length `fullLength`; the executor hands back the full text, and the persisted record separately stores a preview of at most 4,000 characters), and is handed back to the parent task as an untrusted observation; only one is dispatched at a time, and the parent task waits for it to finish; a child task finishing does not mean the user's goal is complete. It is offered only in runs started from the conversation UI and with web capability.

### Records of previous turns

The conversation's context carries only the outcomes of the most recent turns (`Conversation.context`: at most 5 turns, 2,500 tokens), and the original text read in previous turns is not carried. `history_read` (`core/general/HistoryRead.kt`) lets the model look it up itself: without `turn` it lists previous turns (newest first, at most 20: which turn, the user's original words, the outcome, the start time, and whether there is a detailed record); with `turn` (1 = the turn just past) it reads that turn's full outcome, what the user interjected midway, Claire's progress explanations (the text in replies that carried tool calls) and the original text read (the `observed` in the run record, with the same original text given only once), paginated by item (`offset` / `nextOffset`, about 8,000 characters per page, at most 2,000 characters per item), without screenshots. It only recognizes turns of the same conversation brought in at this run's start (`Turn.runId` in `RunSpec.priorTurns`); other conversations and other runs cannot be looked up; it is read-only. The original text comes from the run record (`core/run/record/RunRecords.kt`, on the App side `RunStore.reader()`, read on an IO thread), and was already redacted and truncated when persisted, and is given as-is when read; untrusted things such as web pages are still wrapped in `<untrusted>`. The original text read is handed to verification as an observation (the source is recorded as `history`), so values read earlier and used in the answer can be traced. The run record keeps only the most recent 30 runs, and an earlier turn has only its outcome left (`recorded=false`). It is provided only when there are run records and there are previous turns that recorded runs, and then the context ends with one extra hint line (the history_hint item in `feedback/loop.md`). Child tasks have no previous turns and are not offered it. It can also be called when discussing history (when the intent is judged as discussion).

### Computer connection: CLI and MCP

Two directions. **Claire as an MCP server** (computer connection) is already hooked up, with two ways to connect: over USB (the computer turns it on with adb, listening only on loopback) and over the LAN (the user turns it on in Settings, off by default); **Claire as an MCP client** connects to external servers (Settings › Advanced › MCP servers): `core/cap/mcp/HttpMcpClient.kt` uses Streamable HTTP (2025-03-26), `LegacySseMcpClient.kt` uses the old HTTP+SSE (2024-11-05), and `StdioMcpClient.kt` uses a local process in the sandbox; `McpOAuth.kt` supports OAuth 2.1 discovery, dynamic registration, PKCE and refresh, and a Bearer can also be filled in manually; tools set their risk by annotation and are frozen at run start, and resources/prompts are synthesized into read-only tools. External tool names are uniformly `mcp_<server-id>__<tool-name>`, with invalid characters replaced by underscores; tools longer than 64 characters or whose names collide after cleaning use a short name with a SHA-256 digest. `mcpToolNames` first keeps the declarations that need no renaming, then sorts by original tool name to assign short names; if a short name collides again with any final name, it increases the digest length and recomputes, so it is globally de-duplicated before registration, and a server reordering does not change the mapping. When the server lists the same original name repeatedly, only the first item is kept and the reason for degradation is recorded, while calls still use the uncleaned original name. The setting is in `app/settings/McpServerStore.kt`, with tokens stored encrypted. At the start of a task `McpServerStore.connectionsFor` connects all enabled items in parallel, waiting at most 15 seconds in total (`CONNECT_BUDGET_MS`, separate from a single server's own timeout for tool calls); ones not connected by the deadline are not connected this time and are closed, recorded in the health status (stating they were skipped after the timeout), with a hint on the task card, and it is not counted as a task failure; ones that connect are put into the task's `ResourceScope` on the spot and closed together at wrap-up (ending, cancelling, failing, or being stopped midway through connecting). The external endpoints handed to `toolDispatcher` are just one `mcpServers` list, assembled in one place by `TaskRun.mcpEndpoints()`, and when other sources also provide such endpoints later they will be assembled there. A stdio server's process, while alive, holds a lease of the sandbox tree (`SandboxTree.lease`), not an ordinary shared permit: things that need exclusivity (switching the root filesystem, resetting, clearing the workspace) first stop these processes and take back the permit before acting, without waiting for the task to end and without worrying about missing a close; afterwards calls to that server truthfully report that the connection is closed. The low-level tool session (`core/mcp/McpServer.kt`) is the other direction. For user docs see [mcp.md](../mcp.md) and [cli.md](../cli.md). The computer CLI uses the plain HTTP task API, alongside the MCP adapter, sharing one task service.
The MCP client passes each connected server's `instructions` to the model as bounded, untrusted guidance. The same interface covers Streamable HTTP, legacy SSE and stdio; guidance does not change tool risk or grant user approval.


The **server**'s layers are all in `:core`, and the whole set can be tested on a computer:

- `MiniHttpServer` (`core/remote/HttpServer.kt`): a shared minimal HTTP/1.1 listener. One thread per connection; the connection count, request headers and body all have limits; it understands Content-Length and chunked, and connections can be reused. It keeps the path and query parameters for the entry point to validate; it does no TLS, guarding instead by accepting only private addresses and the access token.
- `RemoteAccess` (`core/remote/RemoteAccess.kt`): the shared gateway for both entry points, in a fixed order: 403 for a request with an `Origin` header; an address that fails authentication 5 times within a minute is blocked for a minute (429, with `Retry-After`); the `Authorization: Bearer` token is compared in constant time, and a mismatch returns 401. Routing and the error format are decided by the adapter, and unknown paths also pass authentication first. `privateAddress` judges loopback, LAN, private-network and link-local addresses; if either end of the connection is not a private address it is disconnected on the spot. The LAN token recognizes all allowed connections, and the USB token recognizes only loopback; both are compared, without short-circuiting. Authentication failure counts are shared across the API and MCP.
- `RemoteTaskService` (`core/remote/RemoteTaskService.kt`): protocol-independent use cases for starting, querying, listing, waiting, answering and cancelling, which validate input and task state in one place and return typed results and business errors; it contains no HTTP, MCP tool names or JSON-RPC envelope. `RemoteTasks` / `RemoteTask` / `RemoteStatus` are in `core/remote/RemoteTasks.kt`; states are mapped only from the existing `TaskState`, and both entry points share fields and the same task index. Waiting returns on state change (a progress-explanation change does not count), at most 50 seconds; if the task in the index disappears it reports that it does not exist. The mcp- prefix is kept for compatibility with existing remote conversation ids and is isolated from local phone conversations.
- `RemoteTaskApi` (`core/remote/RemoteTaskApi.kt`): the plain HTTP/JSON entry point `/api/v1`, providing the version, the task lifecycle, read-only device status, and exporting redacted run records by task id or run record id (`GET /tasks/{id}/export`, with run_export in the capabilities only when there is a record export). No MCP handshake or tool discovery is needed. Business errors are mapped to HTTP status and code/message; unknown fields are rejected. For the detailed contract see [CLI docs](../cli.md#http-contract).
- `McpRpc` (`core/mcp/McpRpc.kt`): JSON-RPC over Streamable HTTP, returning only a single JSON and not opening an SSE stream (the spec allows it); GET / DELETE return 405, no session id is sent, and the server sends no requests. Both protocol generations are recognized: the new generation (2026-07-28) carries the version and client capabilities in `_meta` on every request, and the `MCP-Protocol-Version`, `Mcp-Method` and `Mcp-Name` in the HTTP headers must match the body (mismatch is -32020), an unrecognized version returns -32022 and lists the recognized versions, and `server/discover` is supported; the old generation (2025-03-26 to 2025-11-25) first does an `initialize` handshake and picks a version both sides recognize. Wrong tool arguments or a task that cannot be found are tool execution errors (`isError`), handed back to the model on the other side to see; an unrecognized tool name is a protocol error.
- `McpTaskTools` (`core/mcp/McpTaskTools.kt`): keeps the original tool names, schema, MCP prompt text and return format, and converts arguments into calls to the shared service. `mika_run_task` / `mika_task_status` / `mika_wait_task` / `mika_answer` / `mika_cancel_task` / `mika_list_tasks` all do not store task state separately. Payments, confirmations and phone takeover cannot be let through by an ordinary answer.
- `RemoteEndpoints` (`core/remote/RemoteEndpoints.kt`): the shared protocol routing after transport authentication, holding `RemoteTaskService`, handing `/api/v1` to the plain API and `/mcp` to MCP, and recording recent activity. It does not authenticate connections; the caller first completes the corresponding entry point's authentication.
- `CompanionApi` (`core/remote/CompanionApi.kt`): the three read-only Web console endpoints—conversation list, conversation turns (paginated, truncated) and running tasks; the data is read live by `RemoteCompanion` from the App's conversation storage and task center, without storing state separately or entering the execution path. The gateway side's `/app` page and sign-in session are in another change in `companions/relay`, see [anywhere.md](anywhere.md).
- `RemoteHost` (`core/remote/RemoteHost.kt`): the LAN/USB entry point, holding `RemoteAccess` and the HTTP listener, handing to the shared routing after authentication. `bindAddress` selects all network interfaces or IPv4 loopback 127.0.0.1, consistent with ADB forwarding. The private-address check is not relaxed because of the remote gateway.
- `RelayGateway` / `RelayClient` (`core/remote/`): HTTPS pairing and an active WSS channel, not following redirects, forwarding only fixed MCP POSTs. Requests have independent ids, bounded concurrency and body size; duplicate ids are not executed; cancellation and disconnects interrupt waiting; the connection is re-established with backoff but operations are not replayed. User authorization already verified by the gateway is not passed through as a USB token, and a loopback source is not forged. The tunnel and the local entry point share protocol routing, and tasks are still handled according to the existing remote origin.
- `McpUsb` (`core/mcp/McpUsb.kt`): the convention with the computer over USB. The connection info `mcp-usb.json` adds the plain API's api_url / api_version, and keeps the original content (the Streamable HTTP address, the `Authorization` header, the token, the port, the `adb forward` command, and this boot's number `boot`); "connect-computer.sh" (connect-computer.sh) on the computer: it does not run unless it is shell (uid 2000), first runs "usb-start.sh" (usb-start.sh) once to start the phone assistant, then `am start-foreground-service` to start `McpService` (action `USB_ON`, and `USB_OFF` when it carries the `off` parameter), waits until connection info whose `boot` matches `settings get global boot_count` is written (recognizing that it is not left over from the previous boot), and prints it; when turning it off it waits for the file to be deleted. The script carries no secrets.

**On the App side**:

- Self-hosted remote mode: `RelaySettings` (`settings/RelaySettings.kt`) atomically saves the whole pairing and switch encrypted, with a separate Keystore key; the state flow contains only non-secret fields. `McpRuntime` manages this path independently of the original listener, and when remote is on it reuses the `McpService` foreground service. The notification's close action also closes remote access. Pairing itself does not enable access; after closing, the pairing is kept, and unpairing first closes and then revokes at the gateway. `RelaySection` is the "Remote" item in the MCP guide, using existing components and resources in Chinese, English and Traditional Chinese, and does not display the stored token.

- The LAN kind: the switch and port are stored in `files/settings/mcp.json`, and the access token (a 32-byte random number) is stored encrypted via `KeyCipher` in `mcp-token.bin` like a service provider's Key (`settings/McpSettings.kt`), off by default.
- The USB kind (`settings/McpUsbSettings.kt`): another switch and another token, stored in `files/settings/mcp-usb.json` (together with this boot's number `Settings.Global.BOOT_COUNT` when turned on). Turning it on generates a token; turning it on again while already on reuses the old one; after listening starts it writes the connection info to `mcp-usb.json` in the external files directory (from Android 11 other Apps cannot read it, but shell can; the token has to be handed to the computer in plaintext anyway, so it is also stored in plaintext in the private directory); if it does not start, it deletes it. Turning it off forgets the token (a new one next time) and deletes both files. It does not survive a reboot: if the boot number changed it is treated as off, and last time's files are cleared along the way.
- `McpModes` (`app/mcp/McpModes.kt`): how the two switches combine. Only one port is opened: when LAN is on it binds all interfaces (loopback included, and USB can still connect), when only USB is on it binds loopback only, and when both are off it does not listen; the actions the service receives `USB_ON` / `USB_OFF` / `TURN_OFF` ("Close" on the notification, turning both off) change which switch.
- `McpRuntime` (`app/mcp/McpRuntime.kt`), one per process: when either is on it starts `McpService` (`app/mcp/McpService.kt`, a specialUse foreground service with one persistent low-priority notification that can close it directly), and it listens only while the service is running; each time the service receives a start it `sync`s once: if the listening mode changed (LAN turned on or off) it switches to another listener, and if USB is on it writes the connection info; when both are off it stops the service and closes the port. At App start it writes "connect-computer.sh" once.
- `McpService` is exposed externally and requires the caller to hold `INTERACT_ACROSS_USERS_FULL`: shell has it, an ordinary App cannot get it, and adb cannot grant it (the same gate as the provider for assistant handover), so `adb shell am start-foreground-service -n <package>/.mcp.McpService -a com.kilv.claire.mcp.USB_ON` can only be sent by a computer over USB debugging. It uses a foreground service instead of a broadcast: from Android 12 background Apps generally cannot start foreground services themselves, while a foreground-service start initiated by shell is allowed under the system uid class; if entering the foreground is refused the service exits instead of crashing. A manifest test (`ManifestProvidersTest`) guards this permission. The task service exits by itself when there are no tasks, so the listener is not put in `TaskService`. If the service is reclaimed by the system it is rebuilt (START_STICKY); at boot and when Claire's UI is opened, if the switch is on but it is not running it is started again.
- `RemoteTaskHub` (`app/task/RemoteTaskHub.kt`) implements `RemoteTasks`: `TaskService.startMcp` starts a task, on the same path as the conversation UI, and both CLI and MCP reuse the existing external-task origin `RunOrigin.Mcp`'s permission policy (here a historical name, not meaning CLI goes through the MCP protocol) and presence state `Reachable` (follow-up questions wait as usual, and are treated as unanswered at the deadline; the hand-to-user step has the same deadline, and if nobody handles it past the deadline the task stops). If there is still a task in the same conversation it queues behind it.
  - Receipt records (`app/task/RemoteTaskReceipts.kt`): the `receipts` table of the App-private SQLite database `remote-tasks.db`, one row per remote task: id, conversation, original words, receipt time, whether it opened a new conversation, whether it has been claimed by the task service, terminal state, and where an interjection went. The terminal state and interjection destination are stored as JSON in `RemoteTaskRecord`'s (`app/task/RemoteTaskRecord.kt`) own format, not by serializing `TaskState` / `FailKind` directly: failure categories have fixed names on disk (`gave_up`, `interrupted`, etc.); unreadable ones (bad data, a category written by a newer version) do not throw, an unrecognized category is treated as `environment`, and a wholly unreadable one is returned as a failed terminal state. The answer's evidence ledger is not stored.
  - request_id deduplication: the computer may first choose a lowercase UUID as the request field request_id, and it is the task id. The Hub checks the original request in the same critical section (the original words and conversation parameters must both match, otherwise 409), writes the receipt record, then sends the start Intent; the task service only claims ids not yet claimed (`claimStart`), so concurrent retries and old Intents delivered after the process is rebuilt only start once. Running progress is read from TaskRun, and the terminal state is written to disk before being returned to the computer; if writing to disk fails it is first kept in memory and rewritten on query, and if it still cannot be written it returns 503 to let the computer query later.
  - Interruption: when the database is opened (a new process) receipts that are not yet terminal are marked as failed with category `FailKind.Interrupted`; running remote tasks are recorded the same way when the task service is destroyed. `Interrupted` is like `Environment`: it counts as a runtime-environment matter for scoring, skill trials record it as unreviewed, and learning does not treat it as a failure to do the thing; the reason shown to people uses `task_interrupted` (available in every UI language).
  - Deleting a conversation: this conversation's remote content is cleared, leaving the ids already used as placeholders (keeping the receipt time), and a late retry returns 410 and is not executed again.
  - Retention: cleared once when a receipt is received and once when the database is opened, clearing only finished ones and placeholders—those received more than 30 days ago, and those outside the most recent 1000 by receipt order; ones that are not finished are not cleared. After being cleared, the same id that comes again is treated as a new request, so deduplication holds only within this window. Ids that existed only in memory before the upgrade cannot be recovered.
  - Answers are handed directly to `TaskRun.answer`, stopping is handled through `TaskService`'s `ACTION_CANCEL_MCP`, and queued tasks are removed directly. Only remote tasks registered here can be queried and controlled from the computer. New conversations ask the model for a title as usual.
- Several security guarantees: payments and confirmations can never be let through from the computer—`mika_answer` only hands an answer to a task waiting for a follow-up question; `request_confirmation` rejects directly in runs not started from the phone (`declined_remote`), and `ApprovalPolicy` always Denies payments in runs not started from the phone; runs started by CLI or MCP start in asking mode regardless of the autonomous switch (`ApprovalControl.start(remote = true)`), so autonomous mode cannot let payments through either. On the phone, notifications for questions and takeover still pop up as usual. The run record's start event states the origin (`RunEvent.TaskStarted.origin` = `mcp`, not written for ones started on the phone), and it is also in the summary.
- Settings page (`mcp/ui/McpScreen.kt`): the top distinguishes access not enabled, waiting for a computer request, recently received requests and start failure; a listener or switch being on does not mean the computer is already connected. First choose CLI / Skill or MCP, then choose the USB or Wi-Fi / private network way shared by both. CLI has only the install and connect steps, and connect verifies the connection itself; the network way provides the HTTP root address, the token file command and token copy, while MCP provides the /mcp configuration. Switching the guide does not change permissions. The access switch, token management and remote wake are in the advanced settings collapsed by default; the network MCP configuration preview is also collapsed by default and hides the token, generating the full configuration only when copying. The address and request time refresh every two seconds within the page as before. `ComputerAccessScreenTest` verifies guide switching, explicit permission operations, status and configuration copying.

`mika_steer_task` and `POST /api/v1/tasks/ID/steer` (CLI `task steer`) append a requirement through the same `RemoteTaskService.steer`. It only accepts `running` remote tasks; `TaskRun.steerRemote` then checks the run, pause/wait and cancel states again, and `RunInbox.steerCurrent` is mutually exclusive with close/cancel. A message belongs only to the current task, and if the task ends before it is consumed it is marked as not used, without falling back to the next task the way UI interjections do. It returns the message id and the inbox receipt confirmation; the task state's `steers` lists only remote messages' `received`/`joined`/`not_applied`, without phone interjections or message bodies, and the terminal state keeps a snapshot.

The plain API provides only read-only device status for the CLI doctor; MCP additionally provides the main-screen preparation tools `mika_device_state`, `mika_wake_device`, `mika_unlock_device`. They belong to host device management (the same as pairing and recovery), and are not registered as the model's `AgentTool`, do not enter the model context, action history or credential logs. After MCP's original authentication, `McpDeviceTools` calls `core/device/DeviceAccess.kt`: main-screen state → wake up → request showing the credential page → enter the local credential if necessary → verify the system state. The core depends only on `DeviceAccessPort`, `DeviceAccessStore`; the Android adapter is in `app/device/`, the main-screen input's shell implementation is in `cap/phone/shell/DeviceUnlockInput.kt`, AIDL has a separate method, and the ordinary virtual-screen key whitelist stays unchanged.

`DeviceAccess` has one instance per process and Mutex serialization; remote permission is off by default, and credentials can only be set on the phone. `unlock(authorized)` re-checks source authorization after acquiring the lock and before waking, showing the credential page and submitting credentials, and it can only narrow the local master switch. `DeviceAccessSettings` encrypts and atomically writes the whole configuration with an independent Keystore key and caches only non-secret state. Before input, a persistence failure leaves a placeholder, cleared only when the system is unlocked; after a failure/interruption the credential must be saved again on the phone. The Android adapter only enters on the unique, type-matching password field of SystemUI on the main screen, and does not handle SIM PIN or unrecognizable vendor pages; when the input field does not declare an input type, if the 0–9 digit keys are all present in the same window it is treated as a PIN page, otherwise as a password page; when the input field cannot get focus it first requests focus and then taps once, and if neither works but the SystemUI window it is in is the input-focus window it still counts as typeable; credential keys are injected in a targeted way through `ShellService.injectTo`, delivered only to the focus window owned by SystemUI (`injectInputEvent(event, mode, targetUid)` from Android 13), and when there is another window above the lock screen or it has unlocked back to the App the system refuses, so keys do not land elsewhere, and modifier keys are released to SystemUI only after keys have been sent; before Android 13 it does not enter credentials (`unsupported_keyguard`). The lock-screen gate of `TaskGate` only blocks phone steps: web (private virtual screen) and sandbox steps start and continue as usual when the screen is off and locked, without unlocking; phone steps continue only after the system has actually woken and unlocked, and if it is still locked after automatic unlocking returns, the task ends with the wording for the corresponding source (a locally authorized task that has already done steps changes to a reminder for the user to unlock manually and then continue). When this flow has just woken the screen, `dismiss` retries within the same bounded wait (`waitMs`), re-checking authorization before each try; before unlocking it waits for the accessibility service with `AccessibilityRepair.awaitService`, the same as when connecting to the phone. The settings UI is grouped cards on the computer-connection page, using existing row/input/button components and Chinese and English resources; credentials do not go into restorable UI state, and screenshots are disabled while editing. This feature does not change task sources or the payment policy.

**Read-only screen reading via ADB** (CLI `mika screen`): the computer runs `CLASSPATH=<base.apk> app_process /system/bin com.wangzan101.mika.shell.ScreenInspectMain` through authorized ADB as shell (a stable entry point that only forwards to `app/cap/phone/shell/ScreenInspect.kt`; renaming internals does not affect the CLI), with one line of JSON on standard output and without going through the task service on the phone. It runs only under the shell UID; `UiAutomation` is always connected with `FLAG_DONT_SUPPRESS_ACCESSIBILITY_SERVICES`, so Claire's accessibility service keeps running. A shell process creating `UiAutomation` has to use the hidden constructor and `connect(int)`, probing by reflection one by one, and when something is missing it reports `unsupported` and never falls back to the default connection that would suppress accessibility services (the system's own `uiautomator dump` is that kind). From Android 11 it reads each display through `getWindowsOnAllDisplays` (including Claire's virtual screen), and every window carries `display_id`; earlier versions read only the default screen; windows are ordered with the focused and active ones in front, and the budget goes to them first. Traversal is in `ScreenInspectTree` (pure Kotlin, testable on the JVM): password fields and their child controls do not output text or description; the total number of controls, the depth, each piece of text and the character count of the whole output all have limits, and past them child nodes are no longer taken, `truncated` is true, and `truncated_by` says which item it was. A 15-second watchdog; exceptions report only the category and write no log. The CLI accepts only this entry point's JSON: a missing package, an old APK (`app_process` reports the class not found), an ADB error or output that is not JSON all report `screen_unavailable`, with no retry and no fallback. When another program on the computer is occupying the default ADB server port, the CLI's `--adb-server-port` adds `-P` to all adb calls.

## 7. Context and compression

- **Cache partitions** (`core/agent/ContextBuilder.kt`, `ContextCompressor.kt`): partition 0 is the system prompt and tool definitions, which within a task changes only when a group of tools is expanded (each group at most once, and only the part from that group onward changes), and is otherwise unchanged character for character (except for two kinds of request: the final turn after steps are used up offers only the give-up tools, and the re-ask when judged as discussion offers no tools); partition 1 is the opening context, written once and not changed (previous conversations, memory and profile, the task's original words, available channels, App/website knowledge, and the knowledge and skill index; everything except the user's original words is wrapped in `<context kind=… source=… trust=…>`); partition 2 is history, append-only; partition 3 is this step's result, screen and runtime state (which interface, which step, local time, the beginning of the user's own typed text in their most recent message, appended to the end of the last message). Text the main loop writes to the model (the shell's source and trustworthiness, runtime state, reminders, explanations of confirmation and takeover, replay progress, tool results, folded summaries) is always in English, consistent with the system prompt and tool descriptions, so the model does not answer in Chinese; text shown to the user still follows the interface language.
- **Append-only**: normally messages already sent are not rewritten, and the full history is never modified; only during compression is it rewritten uniformly once on a copy. When `AnthropicModel` sends back assistant messages, blocks stay in the order received and thinking blocks are not moved. Models from Claude 5.1 on also verify the returned thinking blocks: the signature is bound to the system prompt, tools and preceding messages that produced it, and after expanding a group of tools or compressing (and also in the final turn after steps are used up, and the re-ask with no tools when judged as discussion) it no longer matches, and on some accounts and models it returns 400 directly. When rejected, `AnthropicModel` removes the thinking blocks carried this time and sends again, and subsequent requests from the same model instance (one per task, shared by child tasks) no longer carry them (they remain in the history), and anything received after the resend is returned as usual. What checks partition 0 and append-only by the online bytes is `PrefixStabilityTest` (OpenAI-compatible interface) and `PrefixStabilityAdaptersTest` (Responses, Anthropic, Gemini and caching-marking compatible interfaces), running the same 30-step fake task: partition 0 changes only on expansion, and history is rewritten only on compression; the latter also checks that replay's encrypted thinking, thinking signatures and thoughtSignature are carried completely on every step, and that Anthropic carries no more than 4 cache markers in one request.
- **Token budget**: the opening context, memory, previous turns and App/website knowledge are all counted in tokens (`TokenEstimate` rough estimate): total 8,000, Task 4,000, Knowledge 5,000, a single document 2,000; full memory 4,000, retrieval 800; previous turns 2,500 (when only one turn is left and it is still too long, the original words and outcome are truncated separately); previous turns carried by outcome judgement and intent judgement 800. Counting in tokens makes each language take up the same amount of context: compared with the old character-based quota, English can hold about twice as many characters and Chinese about a third fewer; the bundled App/website knowledge documents are all within 2,000 each. During compression the line for a "large result" is 1,000 tokens (the token count corresponding to the old 4,000 characters, with the handling of English and JSON results unchanged). Tools' and the UI's own limits (`maxChars`, `OutputClip`, web read and script results, summary fields, citations, skill docs, etc.) are still by character and have not changed.
- **When to compress**: always by the **whole request** (system prompt + tool definitions + messages, the same measure as the input tokens the provider reports). The size of the whole request is computed by `UsageMeter`: when there is a recent reply from the same model with real usage, = that request's input tokens (already including the prompt and tool definitions at the time) + this reply's output minus the thinking tokens the provider reports separately (`Usage.reasoningTokens`: compatible interfaces' `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, Responses' `output_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, Gemini's `thoughtsTokenCount`; Anthropic does not report them separately, so all output counts) + the estimate for later messages + the change since then in fixed overhead; when there is no anchor (the first step, after switching models, just after compressing) the messages and fixed overhead are fully estimated. Fixed overhead is counted only once. Estimates always multiply by the same "real / rough estimate" ratio: calibrated smoothly once real usage exists, and previously a conservative 1.2. The trigger point is computed in exactly one place, `ContextLimits.of`: available = window − output reservation (at most 20K, and a quarter of the window for small windows); trigger point = available − margin (at most 13K, and a quarter of available for small windows), not below half the window; one compression compresses below "half of available, and one margin below the trigger point". Example: for a 32K window the trigger is 18K, for 128K it is 95K, for 1M it is 967K. After compressing it must grow by at least another margin before compressing again (hysteresis), but never beyond the available window; so history with very large fixed overhead that cannot be compressed is not compressed every step either, and only compresses every step when it truly reaches the edge of the available window. The window and absolute trigger point in Settings are stored per provider and model, assembled through `ProviderStore.modelFor`. An unspecified trigger point is computed automatically by `ContextLimits.of`, and a manually entered value uses the same range. When the provider reports the context too long, it resends once at a more aggressive tier. Three interfaces—OpenAI-compatible, Responses and Anthropic—all count as reporting too long when they return HTTP 400 and the content says the context's length, size, limit or window was exceeded ("exceed", "longer than", "larger than", "greater than", "max" followed in the same sentence by context length / size / limit / window or model length (the wording when a request with images is too long), plus the previously recognized `context_length`, "maximum context" and the old self-hosted service's "context length of only"; merely mentioning words like "context window" does not count, and the error may echo words from the request) (`ModelHttp.contextTooLong`); an OpenAI-compatible streaming request that returns 200 and gives one such error frame in the event stream also counts. Anthropic additionally recognizes 413 and "prompt is too long", and Gemini uses its own rules. When the window is left empty the selected model's context length is used.
- **How to compress** (without calling the model): first shorten old results before the most recent 12 turns (old screen reads are replaced with placeholders, and web, command and file content is truncated only when too long; placeholders keep observation ids), then fold old turns into "Earlier steps (compressed)"; if still not enough, tighten tier by tier. A structured summary (`StepSummary`) is implemented but off by default. When an action carries a screen, the most recent full screen read before the fold boundary is not folded, and the leftover diffs mostly attach after it (the diff reads in between were already replaced with placeholders in the shortening step); when the leftover round-trips' unshortened screens can be understood on their own (at least one, with the `base` of every diff among them), it folds as usual, and folds as usual even under heavy compression. The tier that only shortens and does not fold keeps it as before.
- **Never trimmed**: what the user said (the task's original words, interjections, answers) is never trimmed; the turns of follow-up questions, confirmations and takeover are not folded. After compression `ContextRecovery` puts the missing user messages and opening context (previous conversations, memory, knowledge, skills, channels) back in place.

## 8. Parallelism

Tasks in different conversations can run at the same time, with one virtual screen per task (`app/task/ParallelCapability.kt`): this requires Android 14 and above, with the speed switch "Run different conversations at the same time" turned on (off by default), and a limit of 1–3 (2 by default); on low-memory devices, or once creating a second screen has failed, only 1 is allowed. What is truly exclusive is the same App, main-screen takeover and the clipboard, coordinated in one place by `ResourceScheduler` (`app/task/ResourceScheduler.kt`): the same App belongs to only one task at a time, takeovers queue, the clipboard is locked, and web tabs have a global budget.

**Waiting for another task to release an App**: when opening an App that another task is using, the result is `app_in_use_by_other_task`, and the explanation tells the model it can do something else first, or pass `wait` (seconds, 1–180, `LaunchApp.MAX_WAIT_S`) and call `phone_launch_app` again. Whether to wait and how long is decided by the model per task, with no settings switch. When `wait` is passed and it is still occupied, the tool waits through `ScreenPort.awaitAppFree`: `AndroidScreen` treats "it is not on another screen and no other task holds it in the occupancy registry" as released, and hands it to `ResourceScheduler.awaitApp` (through `TaskCenter.awaitApp`)—which registers "who is waiting for which App", wakes waiters on the spot when a task releases an App (`releaseOwner`, at task end), rechecks every second for Apps on other screens that close themselves without notice, and when released opens it once more, returning `app_wait_timed_out` if it is still occupied at the deadline. Before waiting it follows "the task holding this App is waiting for an App held by ..." all the way; if it comes back to itself they are waiting on each other (the same for cycles of two or three tasks), and it does not wait, returning `app_wait_would_deadlock` (the check and registration are under the same lock, so when two tasks start waiting at the same time the later one sees the earlier one; the earlier one keeps waiting, the later one does not wait and releases the App in its hand when it finishes, and then the earlier one gets it). During the wait the task is still `Running`, with progress `RunProgress.WaitingForApp` (the conversation page, notification and capsule all say "Waiting for another task to finish using an App"), not paused, and not counted as "needs you"; after waiting it is put back to the original line. This is two layers apart from the conversation page's task queue handed to the service (when parallelism is off, if a task elsewhere has started first, the new task queues behind it and starts after it finishes, with "Wait for the previous task to finish before starting" written below the message): that one is the whole task queued behind another task before it starts, running one at a time, so it will not contend for an App; this one is a task already running in parallel waiting for an App midway.

## 9. Experience replay

Successful routes can be recorded, and similar tasks replay them directly, checking lightly at each step without calling the model, and handing back to the model when they do not match (`core/learn/replay/`, with replay actions executed as `Origin.Replay` through the single entry point). Routes are recorded only from runs that succeeded (`LearnGate`, taken from the persisted run events after `TaskRun.settleOutcome` judges the outcome, with `RouteRecorder` only concerned with whether the route itself can be recorded); when replay finishes and this time also counts as succeeded, the route's success count increases by one (`RouteReplay.noteResult`). The speed switch "Do it the way I did last time" is on by default: the old setting is upgraded once by `FeatureFlags` (`speed_defaults_applied_v3`), and after the user turns it off it stays off.

## 10. Scheduling and conditional triggers

- **Tools**: `schedule_task` (`core/general/ScheduleTask.kt`), `op` = create (default) / list / edit / pause / resume / delete / run_now / reschedule. edit reuses PlanDraft/editedTrigger validation, unspecified fields keep their values, and an empty string/empty list or 0 is used to clear explicitly; the host re-checks conversation ownership under the same lock, keeps the plan ID and execution ledger, and returns the next three times. It is offered only in runs started from the conversation UI. When claiming a one-off plan that has been claimed (ran or is running) for resume (`TriggerEngine.setEnabled`) it does not change it and reports `already_ran` (the plans page also gives no switch for such a plan); to run it again use reschedule or run_now.
- **Time rules** (`TriggerDefinition`, `core/trigger/Triggers.kt`): one-off (`at`); fixed interval (`intervalMinutes`, multiple misses are merged into one and the number of misses is recorded); daily or on given weekdays by local time (`repeatAt` or multiple times `repeatTimes` + `repeatZone` + `weekdays`), keeping the same clock time across daylight saving time. Multiple times are sorted and deduplicated uniformly, and preview and advance share nextCalendarAt; when a daylight-saving jump makes two times fall on the same instant it runs only once.
- **Three approaches**: a fixed reminder (`reminder`) is delivered directly at the time and does not call the model; a task is executed at the time as an unattended Run; one with `condition` first checks the condition using read-only web and sandbox, and the result is structured as `ConditionOutcome` (`core/trigger/ConditionOutcome.kt`), with a check error counting not as "not met". The check goal is generated by `conditionCheckGoal` (`core/trigger/ConditionCheck.kt`): the condition text, the comparison baseline, and the requirement to output only `{"met","value","evidence"}`; `value` is the key value to compare next time (format characters removed, whitespace collapsed to one space, at most 80 characters); the baseline is tagged as untrusted content when written into the check goal (`Untrusted`). The baseline (`baselineValue`) is the value at the last dispatched action, and the value first checked when it has never been dispatched. Whether to execute is decided by `afterCheck`: when `notifyWhen` is `ConditionNotify.BecomesMet` (the default) it executes only when the condition goes from not met to met, and when `ConditionNotify.EachTimeMet` it executes every time it is met; when `cooldownMs` is set, the cooldown counts from the planned time of the check that last executed the action (how fast the check runs does not matter), and a check within the cooldown that wanted to execute is skipped and not run later (`lastConditionMet` is untouched), while a check after the cooldown is over executes as usual if met. Skipped periods record the reason `condition_not_met`, `condition_unchanged` or `cooldown`. When the action fails to start synchronously, `revertFire` rolls the dispatch state back to before the check; if there was a baseline before the check it is rolled back too, and if there was none the value recorded this time is kept. After the action has been registered or started, whether it succeeds, fails or is cancelled, the dispatch baseline is kept and the same change is not replayed automatically. The goal of the check run is the generated check instruction; the turn recorded into the conversation (a check error, cancellation while queued, wrap-up after an interruption) and the progress notification use the condition text the user wrote (`StoredRun.userText`), and the check instruction is not recorded as the user's words. `whenIdle` makes it wait until no task is running.
- **Scheduling** (`app/task/TriggerRuntime.kt`): the system alarm is only a wake-up signal, and `FileTriggerStore` is the source of truth. The main plan file keeps the old schema, and the newly added observation values, baselines, notification mode, cooldown and multiple-times list go in a `.conditions` sidecar next to it; older versions can still read the main plan (multiple times degrade to the first time), and sidecar corruption only loses the extension state without clearing the whole plan. Before dispatching it first writes the next time and this window (id = trigger + due time), so the same window is not dispatched twice. A plan whose window has already been recorded in the ledger is no longer claimed, and `rearm` does not schedule an alarm for it either (the condition for judging and for claiming is the same one that checks the ledger, see `slotTaken`): scheduling by an expired due time would fire immediately, fail to claim after firing, and spin forever. It uses an exact alarm when possible; without permission, the first time it sends a notification guiding the user to turn it on in system settings, and the `schedule_task` result also tells the model "it may not be on time"; the plans page shows a hint. It re-arms on boot, time change, time zone change and exact-alarm permission change (`app/setup/BootRestoreReceiver.kt`), and also when the App starts. The device time zone is recorded in `triggers` (`TriggerRuntime.syncTimeZone`): on a time zone change, and when the App starts (also catching up a time zone changed while it was off; the first time it only records the current one), it compares with the recorded one, and if it changed it recomputes local-time plans that follow the old device time zone into the new one (`TriggerEngine.rezone`), while ones explicitly pinned to another time zone are untouched.
- **Result goes back to the original conversation**: a task that is due runs in the conversation that created it, and the result, reminder and failure explanation are all written back to that conversation, and tapping the notification opens it.
- **Run duration and consecutive failures**: "Maximum scheduled task run time" in Settings › Task runs (`FeatureFlags.triggerRunMinutes`, 15 / 30 / 60 / 120 minutes, unlimited by default): a scheduled run (including condition checks) stops after running past this time, the outcome is recorded as `FailKind.TimeLimit`, and like running out of the Token budget it does not count against whether the skill works. Before the result notification is sent, it counts backwards through this plan's own period records how many consecutive failures there were (`failureStreakBefore`; it stops at a delivery or an unclear result, and skips where the condition was not met do not count), and when this one brings it to 3 or more the notification title says "Failed N times in a row", with the end of the body reminding that it can be paused or edited on the scheduled tasks page.
- **Execution with the screen off**: the system alarm is `setExactAndAllowWhileIdle` (falling back to `setAndAllowWhileIdle` without exact-alarm permission; from Android 12 such an alarm may fail to start a foreground service, and if it cannot it truthfully reports a start failure), and the system keeps the CPU up during the alarm broadcast's `goAsync`; the dispatcher `TaskService.startTrigger` first takes `TriggerHandoffWake` (one PARTIAL lock per process, 60-second cap), and releases it once the service has finished handling this start (started, queued, or not started because of pause). When the task starts it takes its own CPU lock on the spot (`TaskService.cpuWake`, `SharedWakeLock` in shares; `KeepAwake(keepThroughEnd = true)` takes and releases it by task state: it is released once the grace period after waiting for your handling has passed, and after a terminal state it is held until the result is delivered and the task coroutine ends); a workflow holds another one between claiming a step and handing it to the task. Kept web pages do not hold a CPU lock. Waking the screen is done only through the unlock flow (the built-in assistant's wake key), and no UI is given turnScreenOn/showWhenLocked.
- **While Claire is paused**: due windows are all recorded as skipped (`PauseFlag.REASON`) and advance to the next one as usual; `whenIdle` ones do not wait for resume either; after resume there is no catch-up run (see §2 "Pausing Claire").
- **Process killed midway**: reminders are delivered again (deduplicated by id); other claimed windows are marked with an unclear result, explained in the original conversation with a notification that "Claire will not repeat the operation automatically".
- **Plans page** (`app/task/ui/PlansScreen.kt`): split into "Active" and "Disabled" sections, each item has three lines (what it does, the repeat rule and next time or state, the last result: skipped ones are split into "condition not met", "condition unchanged" and "within cooldown", shown with the value the check recorded if any), and the switch on the right pauses and resumes; tapping an item raises an action panel: view conversation, run now, edit, delete; the top right can create a new one directly. The editor (`PlanEditorDialog`, whose draft `PlanDraft` is the single source of truth) needs no typing: date and time go through pickers (`PlanTimePicker.kt`, 12/24-hour follows the system setting, and the wording and position of AM/PM follow the UI language, with the time pattern converted from the language's short time format by `PlanTimeFormat.timePattern`), daily/weekly can add multiple times, and quick times count from the moment of tapping; the time zone picker (`PlanTimezonePicker.kt`) shows city names in the UI language, the localized names are indexed once in the background, and it can search by city, id or offset ("+8", "GMT+5:30"); it previews the next three times. The "Run while the screen is off" card (`ScreenOffSetup.kt`, at the bottom of the plans page and in the editor's task mode) read-only lists exact alarms, the battery policy (restricted / optimized / unrestricted, jumping to the system standard settings page) and lock-screen unlock readiness (judged consistently with the branch `RunPhoneStage` actually takes), and the unlock credential is set with `DeviceAccessSection` in a dialog with `SecureFlagPolicy.SecureOn` turned on.

## 11. Knowledge and skills

- **Built-in** (`core/src/main/resources/`): App knowledge (`knowledge/apps/<id>/APP.md`), website knowledge (`knowledge/sites/<domain>/SITE.md`), skills (`skills/<id>/SKILL.md`), one document per directory, so adding one means adding one directory; to see what exists look at these directories or the index generated at build time `knowledge/index.json`, and the list in the README is generated from the same index by `./gradlew updateReadme`. For the format see [`knowledge-format.md`](knowledge-format.md). At build time `KnowledgeIndex` scans the headers, validates and generates the index, and a validation failure fails the build.
- **Automatic supply**: matched by the task text at the start; when entering an App (having read its interface) or a website (opened or read successfully the first time), its knowledge body is added. Each document is given only once, at most 3 per task (`KnowledgeStore`, `NoticePolicy`).
- **Skills are given in two layers** (`GuideRecommender`, `ContextBuilder.start`): the opening index lists all skills, not selected by task text—built-in skills first and personal skills after, each sorted by name, one line each (id, display name, description; a trial description is prefixed to say it has not been verified), with disabled ones and pending drafts not listed. The index is written once in the opening context, and switching to the foreground midway does not add to or change it; it is given whole and not trimmed by the budget. Whether to use one and which one is decided by the model from the descriptions, and to use one it reads the full text with `skill_read`; no skill's full text is stuffed in at the start. Reading a personal skill records a `SkillUsed` (`how` = viewed; the old record's injected is still read and counts as used the same way), and the task outcome is recorded only into the skill that was actually followed this time (`SkillFollow`): after reading it, actions were successfully taken, and if the skill's header names an App / website (the knowledge name, with aliases replaced by registered package names and domains, and App and website knowledge that register each other counted together; unrecognizable ones that do not look like package names or domains count as not written, `GuideCatalog.skillPlaces`) it also must have entered one of them after reading (a screen-read record, or the `currentApp` / `currentSite` reported in a call result); merely reading does not record it, and when more than one was followed and the result cannot be attributed, it does not record either (`RunLearning.recordOutcomes`). Whether to draft also looks only at what was followed: something only read, not followed, but accomplished by itself this time is still drafted.
- **Lookup tools** (`core/general/LookupTools.kt`): `knowledge_search` / `knowledge_read` look up App and website knowledge, and `skill_search` / `skill_read` look up skills (built-in and personal together).
- **Writing a skill at the user's request** (`core/general/SkillSave.kt`, built-in skill `skills/skill-authoring`): when the user asks in a task to create, import or rewrite a skill, the model writes the whole document following the built-in "Write a skill" skill and saves it as a personal skill with `skill_save`. Quotations must come from what the user said this time (text on pages and in files cannot make it write); it goes through the same validation as self-learned drafts (`SkillDrafter.validate`: name, description, five sections, length, tool names, sensitive content); a built-in skill's name cannot be taken, and one with the same name as a pending draft is not accepted; changing an existing one requires the version returned by `skill_read` (the first 12 characters of the original text fingerprint; a mismatch means it was changed midway). Once saved it goes straight into trial and is marked "Written at your request" in "Memory and skills" (`source: user` in `meta.json`), and moves to formal after one success; renaming and deleting stay in Settings. A run that saved a skill this time does not start another self-learned draft (`Eligibility`). What the user said this time (`UserUtterances`) no longer follows the memory switch: it is kept only in memory and cleared at the start of each task, and when memory is off the memory_* tools are still not provided and it is not used to extract memory.
- **Sharing and importing skills** (`core/learn/skills/SkillImport.kt`, `app/skills/ui/SkillsSection.kt`): in "Memory and skills", tapping a personal skill offers "Share skill", which sends the full text through the system share sheet (`SkillImport.shareable` removes the lines that only make sense on this device: `source`, `learned_from`, `updates`). Full text shared by someone else is pasted in from the skill section's "Import skill", without going through the model: `SkillImport.prepare` writes the origin as `source: imported`, passes the same validation as self-learned drafts, cannot take a built-in skill's name, and is not accepted when a draft with the same name is pending confirmation; if a personal skill with the same name already exists it becomes that skill's update draft (with the original text fingerprint, and which lines changed can be seen before accepting). Imported ones always first go into "Pending confirmation" marked "Imported", and start from trial after being accepted. When accepting, an imported draft that has not been changed is validated once more (`MemoryViewModel.approveSkill`; a self-learned draft was already checked against that run's tools when drafted, so an unchanged one is not checked again).
- **Skill marketplace (fork test)**: Settings → Memory and data → Skills → Get skills. Only when the marketplace is opened does it read the `index.json` of the main branch from the selected public GitHub repository; only when an item is tapped does it download `skills/<name>/SKILL.md`. The test version presets `1122414/mika-market-test`, which can be changed in "Skill source". The index has `version: 1`, and each item provides `name/display/description/sha256`; the name, description and content digest must match. The preview shows only text and does not run scripts; adding it to pending confirmation uses the original text previewed this time and reuses the manual import validation.
- **Personal skill self-learning** (`core/learn/skills/`, `app/task/RunLearning.kt`): after a task succeeds (`LearnGate`, which does not require having run final verification), it drafts a personal skill under conditions (enough actions, no skipped takeover, the user did not finish it personally, no declined confirmation, not replayed, see `Eligibility`); when drafting it lists the existing personal skills (together with pending drafts) line by line for the model, and which of them this time's matter is the same kind of thing as is written by the model in the header as `updates: <name>`, with code only checking that it really exists: pointing at an accepted one drafts it as that skill's update (overwriting it when accepted, checked with the original text fingerprint), pointing at a pending draft replaces that draft, and an unmatched name is treated as a new one (a new one cannot take an existing name); the draft goes into trial only after the user accepts it in "Memory and skills", moves to formal when it succeeds during trial, and is disabled on failure; a formal skill is also disabled if it does badly 2 times out of the last 3 (`SkillOutcomes`, with outcomes not judged as done not counted). The switch "Learn skills from completed tasks" is on by default (the old setting is upgraded once when read in `MemorySettings`, and after the user turns it off it stays off).
- **Teaching Claire** (`core/learn/teach/`, `app/teach/`, [`teach-mika.md`](teach-mika.md)): can be entered from the conversation's "+" and the idle capsule. Only after confirming a goal in one sentence and tapping "Start demonstration" does it record taps, long presses, input, scrolling and window switches from accessibility events; consecutive scrolls and inputs are merged. While collecting and while paused there is an accessibility overlay above the screen (`TeachChip`, the same kind of window as the floating capsule, without requesting notification or overlay permission), saying that it is learning or paused, and able to pause or end; if it cannot be added it does not start, and if it disappears midway or accessibility disconnects it stops and records no more. Notifications are only an additional hint. This is separate from `RunLearning` / `LearnGate` / experience routes, does not record Claire's own interface, and does not save whole screens, full text, passwords, verification codes or payment content. At the end the user can correct or delete steps, and it only asks about steps with insufficient information. The draft has `source: demonstrated` and goes into pending confirmation through `TeachPublish.toPending`, not through `skill_save`. Before being accepted it does not enter the skill directory; after being accepted it is still a trial, and when read it requires stopping the application if the interface does not match, and states that it has not yet been run successfully. Giving up, declining, deleting, or deleting after accepting removes the local trace. While collecting or paused it does not start new tasks; after a process restart it does not resume collecting by itself.
- **App knowledge self-learning** (`core/learn/knowledge/AppKnowledgeLearning.kt`, `app/knowledge/AppKnowledgeFiles.kt`): under the same switch, for runs started from the conversation UI that took actions in phone Apps, it judges once per App that had actions and has a built-in knowledge entry (an App without an entry has nowhere to attach the supplement, so it is not learned). There are three kinds worth learning (`AppKnowledgeDrafter.kind`): it succeeded and this App had enough effective actions; it succeeded but with stumbling (two or more actions that errored or caused no screen change, or two or more backs); or it did not succeed or only partly succeeded (judged Partial / NotPossible, or gave up, or ran out of steps) and took at least 3 steps in this App. Cancelled ones, ones that skipped takeover, ones the user finished personally and replayed ones are not learned.
